<meta>This fragment is a continuation of a character dictionary or lexicon, potentially a medieval manuscript of the *Yupian* (Jade Chapters) found in the Dunhuang caves. The text follows a standard lexicographical format: a headword (in large characters) followed by a definition or a phonetic guide in smaller, double-column script (transcribed here as interlinear glosses). Many of the characters belong to categories for birds, plants, and animals.</meta>

<summary>This manuscript fragment serves as a classical Chinese dictionary, providing definitions and Fanqie pronunciations for various terms, including mythical birds, botanical species, and anatomical parts. It exemplifies the rigorous philological traditions of the medieval period, particularly those preserved in the Dunhuang regional caches.</summary>

<meta>This page continues a sequence of dictionary entries from a fragmented rhyme dictionary (韻書). In these traditional Chinese reference works, characters are grouped by their sounds and tones rather than by their radicals. This specific page focuses on several phonetic groups, including "Lou," "Chuò," "Pú," and "Lì." The text is heavily damaged, particularly at the margins, which is characteristic of recovered archaeological or archival manuscripts.</meta>

# Lexicon Fragment (Page 21)

<meta>This page continues the transcription of a fragmented rhyme dictionary from the Dunhuang collection. The previous page (Page 21) covered characters in the "Lou," "Chuò," and "Lì" phonetic groups. # Lexicon Fragment (Pelliot Chinois 4502)

<note>This document is a phonetic dictionary. In ancient China, scholars used these to find the correct pronunciation, meaning, and poetic usage of characters. Pronunciation is often given using the "Fanqie" method: two characters are provided, where the first gives the initial sound and the second gives the rhyme and tone.</note>

**Mò [Northern Tribe]** <gloss>pronounced Mi-Ma <note>original: 靡麻反</note></gloss> **Máo [Banner]** <gloss>pronounced Wang-Ge <note>original: 亡各反</note></gloss> **Méi [Place Name]** <gloss>a town name in the Fengyi district</gloss> **Xú [Slow/Surname]** <gloss>pronounced Hu-Ji; means "different" or "separate" <note>original: 胡及反</note></gloss> **Jì [Sharp/Reap]** <gloss>to harvest; also pronounced Zi-Ru <note>original: 子入反</note></gloss> **Tú [Bitter Weed]** <gloss>pronounced Hu-Ji; a bitter vegetable <note>original: 胡及反</note></gloss> **Rú [Roots/Eat]** <gloss>pronounced Nai-Hu <note>original: 乃胡反</note></gloss> **Luò [Unreel/Network]** <gloss>pronounced Hu-Ge <note>original: 胡各反</note></gloss> **Nú [Inferior Horse]** <gloss>a horse</gloss> **Wú [State Name]** <gloss>the name of a kingdom; commonly written as 吳</gloss> **Wú [Flying Squirrel]** <gloss>resembles a rat; also written as 鼯</gloss> **Luò [River Name]** <gloss>the name of a river</gloss> **Shàn [Good]**

---

**Zhà [Swelling]** <gloss>name of a plant; one version says...</gloss> **Qiú [Jade]** <gloss>pronounced Xian-Qi <note>original: 現琦反</note></gloss> **Míng [Pest]** <gloss>an insect</gloss> **Xiáo [Place Name]** <gloss>a town name in Dongwan</gloss> **Gù [Manacles]** <gloss>shackles</gloss> **Ān [Monkfish]** <gloss>name of a fish</gloss> **Fù [Carry/Rely]** <gloss>a great transgression</gloss> **Cú [To Go/Die]** <gloss>pronounced Hu-Ming <note>original: 胡明反</note></gloss> **Zū [Straw Mat]** <gloss>a reed mat</gloss> **Lǜ [Polish]** <gloss>to grind or polish</gloss> **Lú [Black Soil/Vessel]** <gloss>pronounced Luo-Ming; a vessel, commonly written as 壚 <note>original: 洛明反</note></gloss> **Lú [Brazier]** <gloss>a large furnace</gloss> **Shǔ [Millet]**

It lists characters alongside their meanings and phonetic "Fanqie" guides, covering a diverse range of terms from animals and plants to legal punishments and geographical names.</summary>

<summary>This manuscript page is a fragment of a medieval Chinese rhyme dictionary, organizing characters by their phonetic endings (specifically the Geng and Yang rhymes). <meta>This page continues the lexicographical sequence from the previous leaf, transitioning from the "Huang" and "Geng" rhymes into the "You" (尤) and "Qiū" (秋) rhyme groups. The manuscript remains a fragment of a rhyme dictionary or philological reference, likely from the medieval period, where characters are grouped by their ending sounds to assist poets and scholars. Many of these entries focus on variant character forms and specific regional or historical names.</meta>

<summary>This page contains a sequence of dictionary entries from a rhyme book, providing definitions and "fanqie" phonetic spellings for characters ranging from everyday objects and nature to technical ritual vessels. It demonstrates the sophisticated 7th-century Chinese system of categorizing the language by sound for use in imperial administration and literature.</summary>
<keywords>Chinese lexicography, Qieyun, rhyme dictionary, fanqie phonetics, ritual vessels, classical Chinese botany, 7th-century philology</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the lexical analysis from Page 12. While the previous page touched on garden terms (園, 苑) and phonetics, this fragment delves deeper into the *Shuowen Jiezi* definitions for characters sharing the "wan," "hui," and "hun" sounds, moving toward physical objects like baskets and clothing, as well as anatomical terms.</meta>

<warning>The manuscript is heavily damaged with several large lacunae (holes) on the left side and center-left, resulting in significant loss of text. The paper is darkened and stained, and the ink is faded in several areas. The surviving text is a fragment from a lexicographical work, likely a dictionary or rhyme book.</warning>

# Field Measure <gloss>Grieve; Garden; Bowl; Roll; Coil; Roll; Weary</gloss>
<gloss>Crooked; Luxuriant; Bowl; Roll; Coil; Roll; Weary; Crooked; Garden; Bowl; Roll</gloss>
**Field Measure** (畹) <gloss>Field measure; Garden</gloss> The <term>Shuowen<note>original: 說文; Referring to the *Shuowen Jiezi*, the first Chinese dictionary to analyze character structure, compiled by Xu Shen c. 100 CE.</note></term> says: a field of thirty <term>mu<note>original: 畝; a traditional unit of land area.</note></term>. It is also called a "Garden" (苑). One source says: Crooked; Luxuriant; Garden.
**Grieve** (惋) <gloss>Grieve; Weary</gloss> The *Shuowen* says: to be in pain.
**Scorch** (暅) <gloss>Constant; Bright; Scorch</gloss> <gloss>Glorious</gloss> 
**Scorch** (暅) <gloss>Constant</gloss> The *Shuowen* says: to expose to the sun. One says: to dry.
**Radiance** (暉) <gloss>Splendor; Brightness; Resplendent</gloss> <gloss>Radiance; Splendor; Brightness</gloss>
**Radiance** (暉) <gloss>Radiance; Splendor</gloss> The *Shuowen* says: light. One says: the light of the sun.

# Turbid <gloss>Army</gloss>
**Turbid** (渾) <gloss>Army</gloss> The *Shuowen* says: the sound of flowing water. One says: turbid or muddy.
**Brightness** (輝) <gloss>Brightness</gloss> The *Shuowen* says: light. One says: the light of a fire.
**Resplendent** (韡) <gloss>Resplendent</gloss> The *Shuowen* says: flourishing. One says: the flourishing of flowers.

# Root <gloss>Basket; Basket; To roll; Bowl</gloss>
**Root** (本) <gloss>Basket</gloss> The *Shuowen* says: a vessel made of grass. One says: the root of a plant.
**Basket** (畚) <gloss>Basket</gloss> The *Shuowen* says: a vessel made of grass.
**To Roll** (菤) <gloss>To roll; Bowl</gloss> The *Shuowen* says: to roll up. One says: a bowl made of wood.

# Case <gloss>Fragrant herb; Vine; Apron</gloss>
**Case** (奩) <gloss>Fragrant herb</gloss> The *Shuowen* says: a fragrant herb.
**Vine** (蘞) <gloss>Vine</gloss> The *Shuowen* says: the name of a plant <note>Specifically, a type of wild grape or creeper.</note>.
**Apron** (襜) <gloss>Apron</gloss> The *Shuowen* says: a garment that shields the front <note>similar to a ceremonial apron or flap</note>.

# Large Belt <gloss>Resentment; Resentment; Resentment</gloss>
**Large Belt** (鞶) <gloss>Resentment</gloss> The *Shuowen* says: a large sash or belt.
**Resentment** (慊) <gloss>Resentment; Resentment</gloss> The *Shuowen* says: to harbor a grudge or dissatisfaction. One says: to be satisfied.

# One <gloss>Field measure; Field measure</gloss>

<summary>This page is a fragment of the Qieyun, a classic Chinese rhyme dictionary, featuring character definitions, phonetic guides, and rhyme category tables. --- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the lexicographical sequence of a medieval Chinese dictionary, likely a copy of the *Yupian* (Jade Chapters). Having concluded the sections on jade and physical objects, it now organizes terms by their "radicals" (conceptual categories). This segment covers characters related to "Shells" (representing wealth and currency), "Red" (colors and emotions), "Running" (movement), "The Body," and "Speech." As with previous pages, each entry provides a phonetic guide using the *fanqie* system—a method of combining the initial sound of one character with the final sound of another to indicate pronunciation.</meta>

## **Wealth** <note>original: 賄</note> <gloss>Phonetic: *hu* and *nei* <note>original: 呼内反; This is a *fanqie* pronunciation guide, using the first character's start and the second character's end.</note>. Goods. Riches.</gloss>
## **Relief** <note>original: 賑</note> <gloss>Phonetic: *zhang* and *ren*. To provide for.</gloss>
## **Sufficient** <note>original: 贍</note> <gloss>Phonetic: *shi* and *yan*. Enough.</gloss>
## **Bestowal** <note>original: 賙</note> <gloss>Phonetic: *zhi* and *liu*. To provide for.</gloss>
## **Traitor** <note>original: 賊</note> <gloss>Phonetic: *zuo* and *ze*. To ruin.</gloss>
## **Lowly** <note>original: 賤</note> <gloss>Phonetic: *cai* and *xian*. Low-ranking.</gloss>
## **Sell** <note>original: 賣</note> <gloss>Phonetic: *mo* and *bai*. To put goods out for sale.</gloss>
## **Loan** <note>original: 貸</note> <gloss>Phonetic: *tu* and *dai*. To borrow.</gloss>
## **Credit** <note>original: 賒</note> <gloss>Phonetic: *shi* and *jia*. To give on credit.</gloss>
## **Lend** <note>original: 貰</note> <gloss>Phonetic: *shi* and *zhi*. To loan.</gloss>
## **Secondary** <note>original: 貳</note> <gloss>Means 'two'.</gloss>
## **Adorned** <note>original: 賁</note> <gloss>Phonetic: *bi* and *yi*. To decorate.</gloss>
## **Merchant** <note>original: 賈</note> <gloss>Phonetic: *gu* and *ya*. To sell.</gloss>
## **Tax** <note>original: 賦</note> <gloss>Phonetic: *fang* and *wu*.</gloss>
## **Pledge** <note>original: 贅</note> <gloss>Phonetic: *zhi* and *rui*. A security or hostage.</gloss>
## **Fine** <note>original: 貲</note> <gloss>Phonetic: *ji* and *yi*. A tax or penalty.</gloss>

<summary>This folio fragment from a medieval Chinese dictionary organizes characters primarily by rhyme groups and semantic radicals, specifically listing extensive catalogs of birds and horses. It serves as a vital record of Middle Chinese phonology, using the fanqie system to preserve the pronunciation of characters even where their definitions have been lost to decay.</summary>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This fragment continues a lexicographical sequence from the Yupian (Jade Chapters), one of the most important early Chinese dictionaries. The previous page concluded with terms for familial relations and basic human states. This page transitions through a diverse array of categories: physical injuries, celestial phenomena (the sun), horses, water names, and finishes with a detailed section on birds, specifically wild geese. The manuscript uses the Fanqie system—where the sounds of two characters are combined to show the pronunciation of a third—which I have translated here as "combined with."</meta>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This fragment continues the transcription of a medieval Chinese dictionary, likely a version of the *Yupian* (Jade Chapters). Following the previous page's focus on physical states and nature, this section serves as a phonetic guide (a rime dictionary). It organizes characters by their sounds and provides pronunciation keys using the Fanqie system—a method where the first character provides the initial consonant and the second provides the vowel and tone.</meta>

<summary>This page acts as a phonetic glossary or rime dictionary fragment, likely from the Yupian. It provides the pronunciation for a vast array of characters—ranging from everyday objects like baskets and chickens to abstract concepts like filial piety—using both direct homophones and the Fanqie blending system.</summary>
<keywords>phonology, Fanqie, lexicography, Yupian, medieval Chinese, rime dictionary, Chinese linguistics</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This fragment continues from the previous record of a medieval lexicon. While the previous page touched upon general terminology, this section serves as a phonetic rime dictionary, likely a version of the *Yupian* (Jade Chapters), one of the most significant early Chinese dictionaries. It uses the *Fanqie* system to indicate pronunciation, where two characters are used to "cut" or blend a sound: the first provides the initial consonant and the second provides the vowel and tone.</meta>

<warning>The manuscript is severely damaged, consisting of several fragments mounted on a blue backing. There are massive lacunae (material losses) in the center and left portions. The paper is discolored and stained, though the ink remains generally dark and legible where the paper is preserved.</warning>

## Rhyming Lexicon Fragment (Yupian style)

**Heat** <note>original: 燠</note> <gloss>A blend of *yu* [於] and *liu* [六]; refers to warmth or roasting. Also a blend of *yu* [於] and *liu* [六], meaning to suffer from heatstroke.</gloss> 
**Non-glutinous rice** <note>original: 粳</note> <gloss>A blend of *gu* [古] and *xing* [行].</gloss> 
**Sunstroke** <note>original: 暍</note> <gloss>A blend of *yu* [於] and *xie* [歇]; to be harmed by summer heat.</gloss> 
**Ankle** <note>original: 踝</note> <gloss>A blend of *hu* [胡] and *wa* [瓦]; the bone of the foot.</gloss>

### Flesh Radical Section (肉部)
<note>This section organizes characters containing the 'meat' or 'body' radical, primarily relating to anatomy or food.</note>

**Fetus** <note>original: 胎</note> <gloss>A blend of *ta* [他] and *kai* [開].</gloss>

<summary>This highly damaged fragment records the transition to the "Daye" era (605 CE) under Emperor Yang of the Sui Dynasty. It likely served as a formal record of imperial decrees or administrative reorganization of prefectures during that period.</summary>

<keywords>Sui Dynasty, Emperor Yang, Daye Era, Imperial Decree, administrative history, chronology</keywords>
